[40:03.52] What is PowerBI desktop? PowerBI desktop
[40:06.24] is a free application for your local
[40:08.24] computer that allows you to connect to,
[40:10.56] transform, and visualize data. It
[40:12.88] enables you to connect to multiple data
[40:14.80] sources and combine them into a data
[40:16.80] model. This data model allows you to
[40:19.20] create visuals and collections of
[40:21.04] visuals that can be shared as reports
[40:23.28] with others within your organization.
[40:25.84] Most users who work on business

[100:19.52] Now let us understand what is SQL in
[100:22.80] PowerBI. Well, structured query language
[100:25.84] in PowerBI extracts, transforms, and
[100:28.40] manages data before visualizing it in
[100:30.48] reports and dashboards. PowerBI connects
[100:33.12] to SQL databases, allowing users to
[100:35.28] fetch relevant data, optimize queries,
[100:37.60] and enhance performance. But why SQL is
[100:40.64] crucial for PowerBI? SQL plays a vital
[100:43.60] role in PowerBI by enabling efficient
[100:45.76] data extraction, transformation, and
[100:47.92] optimization before visualization. Since
[100:50.48] PowerBI is a datadriven tool, having
[100:52.96] clean and well structured data
[100:54.32] significantly improves performance and
[100:56.32] reporting accuracy. SQL helps query
[100:59.28] large data sets, perform complex joins
[101:01.76] and aggregations and optimize data loads
[101:04.56] before bringing them into PowerBI. Now
[101:06.96] let us see some of the key reasons. So
[101:09.36] first here we have efficient data
[101:11.52] extraction. SQL allows you to fetch only
[101:14.24] the necessary columns and rows reducing

[287:32.16] Now let's start with pageionated reports
[287:34.96] and what are they? I assume some of you
[287:38.00] might have already used SQL server
[287:40.48] reporting services. For them,
[287:42.24] pageionated reports are not a new thing
[287:44.96] for people for which this concept is
[287:47.12] new. These reports are called
[287:49.12] pageionated reports because they are
[287:50.96] formatted to fit well on a page. They
[287:53.92] display all the data in a table even if
[287:56.32] the table spans multiple pages and hence
[287:59.04] given the name pixel perfect reports
[288:01.36] because you can control the report page
[288:04.32] layout exactly and precisely as you need
[288:06.72] it to be. Pagionated reports are based
[288:08.96] on the RDL report technology in the SQL
[288:11.36] server reporting services and can have
[288:13.92] many many pages to begin with. Now
[288:16.56] pageionated reports have their own
[288:18.56] design tool which is in fact known as
[288:21.28] the PowerBI report builder. The tool
[288:23.84] we'll be working on today. It's a new
[288:26.24] tool that shares the same foundation as
[288:28.16] the tools you'd previously used to
[288:30.40] create the pageionated reports if you
[288:32.48] have done so. In fact, for those of you
[288:34.96] who have used SSRS before, pageionated
[288:38.16] reports that you created there in 2016
[288:40.88] or 17 or in the PowerBI report server on
[288:44.80] premises services are all compatible
[288:47.28] with the PowerBI service. PowerBI
[288:49.60] service thankfully maintains backwards
[288:52.08] compatibility. So you can move your
[288:53.68] reports forward and then you can upgrade
[288:55.68] any previous version pageated reports.
[288:58.00] So report builder is your standalone
[289:00.32] tool for authoring pageionated reports.
[289:03.04] Now a single pageionated report can have
[289:04.88] a number of data sources. It doesn't
[289:07.28] have an underlying data model unlike
[289:09.60] your PowerBI reports. So for the initial
[289:12.16] release of pageionated reports in
[289:13.84] PowerBI service, you create embedded
[289:16.48] data sources and data sets inside the
[289:18.80] report itself. You create reports in
[289:22.00] report builder on your local system. And
[289:24.48] if a report connects to on premises data
[289:27.52] after you upload the report to the
